# M3, the reading layer, the rest of the safety machinery and the kit: hand-off plan

The plan for the third milestone: write fresh, in this repository, everything a person or agent reads in order to work, finish the safety machinery, and package the workspace as a kit an instance can adopt. Building sessions work from it; the maintainer approves it. It follows the [hand-off plan template](../templates/hand-off-plan.md).

**In short:** the work workspace holds about 60 core procedures, templates and record types, and machinery this repository lacks. This plan ports them in three sub-batches: the eval harness and the page structure first, with one recording; then the procedures, as pages marked proven or unproven; then the machinery and the kit. A closing slice makes the queued contract and index edits, records the eval once more, and measures whether the new pages harm what is proven.

## Key points

Eighth draft, after rounds 1 to 7 of the red team. Every accepted action of the seven rounds is applied to the body. The seven choices that needed the maintainer's yes are settled (see "Settled decisions"). Round 7 only finished earlier work, so under the program's stop rule the review loop ends there. The maintainer approved the plan on 2026-11-18.

- **A playbook can now ship unproven, but says so.** Today every page in `docs/playbooks/` must have an eval case that beats the bare model. Of the about 60 pages this plan ports, about half are templates, record formats and tutorials, which checks and CI hold instead. About 29 are procedures an agent follows. Proving those now is not honest: cases must come from real tasks, this workspace has two milestones of them, and many procedures need machinery from M4 or M8 before they can run. Instead every procedure carries a `proof` field. A `proven` page needs a real-run record and a passing eval record. An `unproven` page says so in its front matter, which agents and people read. The eval runner strips the proof keys, in their exact forms only, before a gated arm reads a page, and the hash ignores the same text, so marking a page proven does not stale its own record. The gate for proven pages gets stricter, not looser: each must beat the two-line arm on its own. Decision record 0006 sets this.
- **Unproven is temporary.** Each unproven page names the milestone that is to prove it, in a `prove-by` field. A check fails an unproven page once that milestone's record is signed off. Moving `prove-by` later is refused unless a failed proof attempt is on record: a record under `evals/runs/` that names the page and the milestone it moves away from, with the verdict "not proven", made while that milestone was being built. B2 sets each page's `prove-by` in a table: the milestone whose program section runs that procedure on real work, with M6 as the default for development procedures. The six procedures that may be model coaching get `prove-by: M4`, and the program's M4 carries their proof attempts. Each later milestone a page names carries that page's proof attempts and their run budget in the program.
- **Shipping unproven pages must not hurt what is proven.** M2 showed that added text can: a longer index dropped the guided arm from 45 to 40 of 45. The closing slice adds a fourth arm, "routed": the guided arm's files, every page the index routes to, and the skill wrappers every session loads. It must pass at least the guided arm's count minus 3, pooled across the three cases, or the plan stops. It is measured once, in a record the gate never reads, because a gate would make every doc edit cost a recording; the README says so.
- **The guided arm is also held to its own past.** A new recording whose guided arm passes more than 3 runs fewer, pooled, than the previous record, or than the record `fix-a-bug.md`'s `proven-by` names, fails. Today's gate cannot catch a slide like M2's 45 to 40. Comparing with both records stops small slides from adding up: 45, 42, 39 would pass a check against the previous record alone. The anchor is M2's closing recording (40 of 45), not M1's 44: anchoring at M1 would fail R1 for a slip M2 already recorded and published. Each recording overwrites a case's record file, so the anchor is pinned to a commit, and a check refuses moving it without an approved change.
- **The margin tests can stop the plan on noise, so a miss is recorded once more.** At M2's rate of 40 of 45, a 45-run arm falls more than 3 short of an unchanged one about 12% of the time. On a margin miss, that arm is recorded once more, and the pooled 90 runs are judged at a margin of 6. Simulated at M2's rate with no real change, the chance that R1, R2 or the routed arm stops the plan falls from about 30% to about 15% (computed, 200,000 trials). That 15% is the false-stop rate this plan accepts. It depends on the true rate: about 7% at 0.93, about 35% at 0.85 (computed exactly). The other side is weak too. A routed arm that truly drops to 80% is caught about half the time, so a pass is not proof of no harm, and the report says so. The anchor of 40 makes 37 of 45 a floor that later records may sit on; that is a known limit.
- **The public sentence "a playbook stays only while it beats the bare model" stops being true as written.** In this repository it lives in the `eval_record` entry's `why` in `workspace.json`, the part's docstring and the README. Slice A3 changes each to "a playbook marked proven stays only while it beats the bare model", beside the README's count of proven and unproven pages. Pages outside this repository are out of scope.
- **Every harness change lands before the first recording.** The eval hashes the harness into every case, so a harness change after R1 would stale every record. A0 builds the routed arm's support, every grader, per-case arm files that replace the shared ones, and the shared shell parser's move into `wslib/`, with every command form the new guards must see.
- **Sub-batch A merges as one, with R1's records.** The machinery page's trap allows no pull request until the commit holding the eval records passes every hook, and `eval_record` fails every case once the harness changes. So A0 to A4 share one branch and one pull request. C1, C2 and C7 need none of A's code and start from main at once.
- **Recordings and their cost.** R1 after the harness and structure changes; R2 at close, with the routed arm in a separate session. Proof attempts for the adversarial-review and debugging playbooks, each starting with a bare-only baseline, at 22 runs per case so one lost run cannot sink the gate, with the baseline and the recording in separate sessions. Planned runs: 619 agent runs on the small model, plus about 150 short selection runs, at most 150 per session. A margin re-record adds 45 runs, so all three re-records make 754. R1's retry follows R1's own re-record, so that path plans 799. Probes, failed runs and the sessions an adversarial-review run dispatches also count: about 30 to 65 probes, and about 90 to 150 dispatched sessions. Counted that way, the base path is about 740 to 840 runs and the retry path about 925 to 1,030. The eval stop is 1,000 runs. So on the retry path the session projects the remaining runs when the retry is chosen, and may then defer B7's adversarial-review recording to M9, its `prove-by` milestone. It projects again before R2, and if the projection still passes 1,000, the plan stops for the maintainer's decision rather than start a recording it cannot finish. On the retry path, the retry gets no re-record, and a margin miss in R2 or the routed arm is a stop, not a re-record.
- **R1 also tests M2's open question, but cannot settle it alone.** The guided arm slipped from 45 to 40 of 45 after M2's index grew, and 17 runs went looking for `docs/rules/`. R1 gives each case's guided arm the rule pages its code paths match. It changes the contract and index in the same recording, so the result is confounded, and the report says so.
- **Wrappers only for what can run now.** Each core procedure that can run today gets a skill wrapper. Lane drafts, and the core pages that need M4's review machinery or M8's providers, get none yet: a wrapper's description loads into every session and would route agents to a procedure that cannot run. The wrapper check lists the waiting pages by name.
- **The always-loaded layer gets measured ceilings.** The contract and tool pointers stay under 6,000 bytes (3,646 today), and the wrappers' descriptions under 9,000 bytes in all. These are ceilings, not targets. The routed arm's result sets the final budget, recorded in `m3.md`.
- **The shell guards get the facts they decide on.** Today the before-shell event carries only the command. An amendment to decision record 0004 adds the working copy the git call acts on, the session, the lease holder and the target repository, before any guard that needs them.
- **The outward-write guard refuses every write on other repositories.** Agents here may publish and merge their own pull requests after CI passes. The new guard refuses approval everywhere. On a repository not in the instance's own list, `gh` may run only a named list of read subcommands. Everything else is refused, including `gh api` calls that write and `git push` to that repository. The guards work out where a command really goes, from git's and gh's own configuration, instead of listing command forms. Only this repository is listed, and an adopted instance starts with an empty list. The guards refuse an agent's change to the list, by an edit tool or by a shell write; a person edits it from a terminal.
- **One plan, approved once, in three sub-batches.** The program asked for one plan per sub-batch; one plan with three sub-batches keeps a single red team and a single approval. Any change after approval is a dated entry under "Changes after approval", approved one by one as template rule 6 says. Edits to hashed files that wait for slice Z are not changes of plan: they go in a list under "Results".
- **Builders read the private port catalog, never the work workspace.** The catalog was written behind the wall and holds generic descriptions only. B0 builds a private ledger that maps every catalog item to a page or a reason, and orders one walled-reader pass for the entries too thin to build from, before any page is written.

## Settled decisions

The maintainer, 2026-11-15:

- The plan is written so the workspace itself builds it, which makes building it a test of the workspace.
- The workspace has many areas to expand before testing begins, and the import from the work workspace was narrower than intended.
- The workspace officially covers and guides automation, DevOps and product work, not only development.
- The workspace does not compete with anyone. Other setups are cited only as credit for an idea taken from them.

The maintainer, 2026-11-16, approving M2's plan, carried here:

- Builders never open the work workspace's files. A walled reader session, which builds nothing, reads them and writes generic descriptions.
- Eval-harness work is committed with `--no-verify` before the paid runs; this is a standing rule.
- From M2's slice 4 on, slices are built in worktrees under `.claude/worktrees/`.
- Eval runs are paid from the maintainer's existing subscription.

The maintainer, 2026-11-17:

- The port catalog's batch table is built as written.
- Six procedures that may be model coaching stay in: test-first, debugging, the throwaway prototype, delegation, safe refactoring and the research ladder. The proof rule decides them.
- One tag per milestone: M3's is `v0.4.0`.
- Yes to red-team finding 18: the request door and the release-and-rollback drafts leave M3. M9 and M11 build them from real work, and the guide names them as planned.
- Yes to red-team finding 37: the discard guard refuses only while another live session is on record, which keeps decision record 0005's promise to a person working with one session.
- Yes to round-2 finding R2-22, deferring both items: verifying a change on the local environment waits for M5, and machine-state records wait for M4 or M5.
- Yes to round-2 finding R2-23: M4 carries the proof attempts for the six procedures that may be model coaching. The program's M4 section now says so.

The maintainer, 2026-11-18:

- Yes to round-3 finding R3-1: the eval runner strips the proof keys from a page before an arm reads it, the hash ignores them, and the unproven label lives in those keys only.
- Yes to round-3 finding R3-13: each later milestone that a page's `prove-by` names carries that page's proof attempts and a run budget. The program's M5, M6 and M8 to M11 sections now say so.
- Yes to round-4 finding R4-1: the eval stop rises from 800 to 1,000 agent runs, with probes, failed runs and dispatched sessions counted.

Carried from M1 and M2:

- Agents write on no system other people can see, and nothing private goes into this repository.
- An eval result counts at 40 or more trials per arm.
- In this repository agents push, open draft pull requests, and publish and merge them once CI passes on Linux, macOS and Windows.

## Goal

Everything a person or agent reads in order to work here exists, written fresh, and each procedure says whether it is proven. The safety machinery M2 did not build is in place, with cases and CI on three systems. A new instance can adopt the kit, and a drift check catches a hand edit to a file the kit owns.

## Non-goals

- No trap tables for one tracker or one git host, and no provider adapters. Those are M8.
- None of the six procedures and six pieces of machinery the catalog leaves out, and nothing the maintainer's own records retired: the prompt-nudge hook, the unattended loop harness, stored knowledge-graph output, the kit health check, prompt wrappers for a second tool, and code-map freshness as a blocking gate.
- No environment slots, code maps, architecture model, machine-state records, review machinery, or page on verifying a change in a local environment. Slots, code maps and that page come with the first instance in M5; machine-state records come in M4 or M5; review machinery is M4. The maintainer agreed to both deferrals on 2026-11-17.
- No request door and no release-and-rollback draft. The catalog has no source for either; M9 and M11 build them from real work.
- No edit to a file the eval hashes, except in sub-batch A and the closing slice. The list is whatever `covered_files` in `parts/eval_record.py` returns for each case. Today that is `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md`, `INDEX.md`, `docs/playbooks/fix-a-bug.md`, `docs/definition-of-done.md`, `parts/no_stage_all.py`, `evals/arms.json`, `evals/two-line.md`, the harness, the cases, their acceptance tests and their scaffolds. A0 adds the rule pages and the shared shell parser. One exemption: B7 may add and edit files that are hashed only into a case B7 adds. A slice that needs any other such edit queues it for the closing slice, in the list under "Results". That includes a fix to the shared shell parser: a guard that finds a parser gap handles it in its own part file, outside the hash, until slice Z.
- No third-party packages in the engine, the parts or the tests CI runs.
- No outward writes beyond this repository: no upstream repository, no issue, no comment.
- No page copied or paraphrased line by line from the work workspace, and no re-sync with it after this milestone.
- No instance repository. The kit is proven on a scratch instance; the first real one is M5.
- No wrapper for a lane draft, or for a core page that cannot run before M4 or M8.

## Why this needs a plan

It fails two size-gate conditions: it is far more than one screen of diff, and far more than a day of work. Several slices change gates, and a broken hook can lock a session out.

## Slices

Every slice is built in its own worktree under `.claude/worktrees/`, on a branch `m3/<slice>`, except sub-batch A, whose five slices share the branch `m3/a` and one worktree. A slice that touches a machinery path merges through a pull request; a doc-only slice may push straight to main from its worktree, as [changing the machinery](../changing-the-machinery.md) allows. The tier comes from the [orchestration rules](../orchestration.md): every slice that changes a gate, a hook, a grader, CI or the contract, and every review, runs at the driver tier.

### Sub-batch A: the harness and the structure, with one recording

| Order | Slice | Work | Tier | Done when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A0 | The harness, generalized | A case may name its own arm files. They replace the shared arm files and `arms.json`'s entries for that case, and are hashed into that case only, so a case that names its own depends on no shared arm file. An arm may be marked one-time: it is defined outside every gated case's hash, so adding it stales no record, and its runs go to a record `eval_record` never gates. An arm may enable the wrappers' local plugin inside the eval's sandbox. Before a gated arm reads a page, the runner strips the proof keys and section proof lines in their exact forms only: `proof: proven` or `proof: unproven`, `prove-by: M<n>`, `proven-by:` followed by a comma-separated list of bare `<sha>:<path>` or repository paths with no link text, and the section lines A3 defines. The hash ignores the same text through the same function, which lives in a harness file `HARNESS` names; `eval_record` loads it from the snapshot's text, as `grade.py` loads hook files, so the hashed text is exactly the text tested. `page_kinds` fails any other form of those keys or lines, so a variant is hashed, not hidden. Cases show that a proven/unproven flip leaves a record current, and that an extra word on a proof line or link text on a `proven-by` line stales it. The one-time routed arm reads pages unstripped, as they ship. A test lists each B7 case's `covered_files` and fails on any file criterion 61 does not allow. The routed arm is defined here, before R1. The three fix-a-bug cases' guided arm also gets every rule page in `docs/rules/` whose `applies-to` matches a path the case's acceptance test or graders name. A bare-only baseline mode writes a record the gate never counts as passing. A per-playbook test pools a playbook's cases and requires guided to beat two-line, one-sided Fisher exact, p < 0.05, with 40 or more runs per arm. A new record whose guided arm passes more than 3 runs fewer, pooled, than the previous record's, or than the record the playbook's `proven-by` names, fails; the engine gives the part both records: the previous one from history, at the commit as in CI, and the anchor from the commit-pinned path `proven-by` names, `<sha>:evals/runs/<case>/record.json`. With no base, as in a plain `check` or the scheduled CI run, the comparisons skip and say so. A record may pool one re-record of an arm that missed its margin: `judge()` accepts that arm with twice `runs_per_arm` runs and, when arm sizes differ, compares pass rates, with the Fisher test on the real counts, and the pooled 90 runs are judged at a margin of 6 against twice the earlier count. Every comparison puts both sides on the 90-run scale: a 45-run count is doubled when the other side is pooled, and the margin is 6 when either side is pooled and 3 when neither is, the case where both are pooled included. Nothing is halved. A margin runs over the cases present in both records. Graders for the adversarial-review protocol (a fresh session was dispatched, the findings file exists, the reply gives only its path and verdict, a triage table exists) and for a diagnosis (a regression test fails on the original code, the debug output is gone). The shared shell parser moves from `no_stage_all.py` into `wslib/shell.py`, with a row in decision record 0004 for each part that loads it, and the hash covers it wherever a case grades a hook. The parser learns every form the new guards must see: `env` and `printenv` as commands, `GH_REPO` and `--repo`, `git -c alias.…`, `+` and `:` refspecs, `--mirror`, `-f`, `gh api` with its method, its field flags, `--input` and its path, `gh api graphql` under each endpoint spelling (`graphql`, `/graphql`, an absolute URL, and with a query string), `-R`, `GH_HOST` and `--hostname` (an unknown host is unresolved), `git push` to a remote or a URL, `git -c`, `git config` and `GIT_CONFIG_*` or `GIT_CONFIG_PARAMETERS` writes to `remote.*`, `url.*`, `push.*`, `branch.*.pushRemote`, `branch.*.merge`, `remote.pushDefault`, `include.*`, `includeIf.*`, `alias.*`, `core.worktree` and `core.sshCommand`, `GIT_SSH` and `GIT_SSH_COMMAND`, `git remote add` and `set-url`, `find -exec`, `-execdir` and `xargs` as wrappers whose supplied arguments are unresolved, `switch -f`, `checkout -f`, `read-tree -u --reset`, `reset --keep`, `--autostash` or `rebase.autoStash` on the rebase family, `stash drop`, `pop` and `clear`, and `worktree remove` and `prune`. It resolves the folder a git call acts on from `-C`, `--git-dir`, `--work-tree`, `GIT_DIR`, `GIT_WORK_TREE` and a leading `cd` or `pushd`. A `cd` or `pushd` that is not the leading word, a `cd` in a subshell, `core.worktree`, `GIT_INDEX_FILE` and `GIT_COMMON_DIR` leave the folder unresolved. It reads inside `$(…)` and backticks. A command is unresolved when its program word is a variable or a substitution, or when text is piped into a shell. Beyond that, each guard judges only its own commands: a command it decides on (the force-push guard a `git push`, the outward-write guard a gh call or a push, the discard guard its listed forms) is unresolved when its git or gh subcommand, or a guarded argument (a refspec, `--repo`, `-R`, `GH_REPO`, `-C`, its target folder), is a variable or a substitution. So `cd "$D" && make` passes every guard. A command the discard guard decides on whose folder is unresolved is itself unresolved. The parser also reports each command's write targets: `>`, `>>`, `tee`, `cp`, `mv`, `sed -i`, `dd of=`, `rm`, `ln`, `truncate`, `install`, `rsync`, and `mv`'s source as well as its target. Message bodies (`-m`, `--body`, `--title`, `git commit -F` and gh's `--body-file`) never make a command unresolved; `gh api -F` is a field, not a message body. Re-grading the stored transcripts gives no difference. | driver | Criteria 1–6, 55–56 |

A0 goes first, so the recording covers the new harness. A1 to A3 go before R1 because each edits a hashed file. Sub-batch A is one branch: its commits use `--no-verify` until R1's records are in, as the [machinery page's trap](../changing-the-machinery.md#traps) requires, and then one pull request merges A0 to A4 together. Because those commits skip the private-term scan, every commit message and diff on `m3/a` passes `ws.py check --message` and the deny-list scan before every push of `m3/a`, since a pushed branch on this public repository is visible at once; the machinery page's trap gains that step. C1, C2 and C7 touch no hashed file and need none of A's code: they branch from main and start at once, each with its own pull request. If one of them finds it needs A's code after all, it waits for A to merge. Everything else in sub-batches B and C starts after A merges.

### Sub-batch B: the reading layer

Built from the private port catalog's descriptions. Every page lands with the front matter A1 requires and the `proof` field A3 requires. Pages are written in batches of about ten. The maintainer reads each batch after it lands. A page they strike is removed in one commit together with its wrapper, index rows and table row; its ledger row is not deleted but changed to "struck by the maintainer" with the date, so the item stays mapped. Building does not wait for the read.

| Order | Slice | Work | Tier | Done when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| B0 | The port ledger | A private ledger beside the catalog, outside every repository, with its own check. It maps every item the batch table gives M3, each of the 40 lessons, each decision, each disagreement found, the three ideas kept from left-out items, and every item the two red-team rounds found missing to a public page, a slice, a later milestone, or a reason. One walled-reader pass deepens the entries too thin to build from: the fix brief's sections, the test-matrix axes, the handoff note's sections, the unit-doc headings per kind, the layout's fixed headings, the security-review surfaces, and the two one-line entries. Built from the catalog only. | worker | Criteria 16, 24, 63 |
| B1 | The instruction-file review, first | The review that treats guidance as a program: contradictions, executable steps, triggering text, enforcement of every must and never, placement and load cost, an eval case for new behaviour. A fresh driver-tier session runs it and writes a record of its findings, by class, in the records folder. The commit names the record in an `Instructions-review:` trailer, or gives a skip reason from a listed set: link or typo only, front matter only, or a strike. A commit-message check refuses a commit that adds or changes a guidance page without one, or names a record that does not exist. A local pre-push check refuses a push whose commits lack it, since a doc push lands on main before CI runs. CI runs the message checks over every commit of a pull request, and over every push to main, where it can only report a failing run after the push. The review's first run covers the guidance sub-batch A wrote before the review existed: the contract and index edits, the layout page, the glossary, decision record 0006 and the rules tables, each with a record. Every later page in this milestone goes through it, and the stop rule's count comes from the records. An index of review records lists the page each covers, and a check holds it to criterion 62's list. The slice also adds the CI test that runs every command block a tutorial marks as runnable, in a fresh clone on three systems; it passes with no tutorials yet and bites once B2 adds them. Since a tutorial edit goes straight to main, the local pre-push check also runs the runnable blocks of any tutorial a push changes. | driver | Criteria 17, 20, 62 |
| B2 | Core procedures | The fix path with its five human gates, the feature ladder, the spec process, the decision interview, adversarial document review, test-first, debugging, the throwaway prototype, safe refactoring, the research ladder, delegation, impact analysis, the reuse checklist, the review orchestrator's page and its explainer (its machinery is M4), architecture-invariant review, reviewing a pull request in an isolated worktree, escape analysis, the feedback walk-through, the tracker and git-host spines, the docs audit, the prompt retrospective, handoff, extending, maintaining and adopting the workspace (the adopt page records where per-repository docs live as an open question), the machine self-check prompt, two tutorials, the playbook catalog, the how-to index, and the one-line request map. All `proof: unproven`. Pages that overlap one already here merge into it instead of standing beside it: the fix path's gates that can run now into a section of `fix-a-bug.md` with its own proof line, queued for slice Z; the feature ladder and the spec process into `size-the-work.md`; delegation into `orchestration.md`. Each page takes its `prove-by` from the table below this one; a merged procedure carries its own section proof line, so the page it joins keeps its own state. The explainer is a reference page and carries no proof field. | worker | Criteria 16, 20 |
| B3 | Templates and records | Plan, spec, hub and unit-doc templates, with the investigation, audit, retrospective and baseline variants of the spec; the bug ticket and pull request standards, with the bookkeeping list after a merge; authoring conventions. The plan template merges into `hand-off-plan.md`. The definition of done is queued for the closing slice, because the eval hashes it. Record types: fix record, review record, interview summary, adversarial review record, epic folder, handoff note, fresh-session prompt, defect register, lessons log. The existing question log gains its gap kinds and the kickoff prompt a newcomer starts from. A `claim_marks` check fails a fix record whose report holds a claim line not marked verified or inferred. | worker; driver for the check | Criterion 18 |
| B4 | Lane drafts | QA: the regression case and report formats, run and triage, the QA steps document, the candidates ledger, the correction ledger. Product: grooming, epic satellites, the standup report and its log, the effort estimate. DevOps: importing a repository, the environment self-check, shipping a new deployable. Automation: saved workflows. Development: the feature layer walk. Each carries `lane:`. Each lane playbook and how-to also carries `proof: unproven`, with `prove-by` set to the milestone whose program section runs it on real work: by default the lane's proving milestone (QA M6, product M9, automation M10, DevOps M11, the feature layer walk M9), and M10 for the regression-suite pages, which M10 runs. Record formats and templates carry no proof field. A guide gives each of the five roles a reading path. The product path names the request door as planned for M9, and the DevOps path names release and rollback as planned for M11. | worker | Criterion 19 |
| B5 | Decisions and lessons | Each of the sixteen workspace decisions the catalog marks core or lane becomes a public decision record, or amends one already here, in the catalog's record format. One record settles every place where two source pages disagreed, with the choice for each: the catalog's list of ten, and an eleventh found elsewhere in it, where the catalog's decision on spec-driven work says specs persist and change with the code, while its spec page kind says a spec is frozen once written. The same record lists each deliberate departure from the catalog, such as the instruction review at the driver tier with a trailer the commit check requires, where the catalog has a worker-tier review and a hook that only warns. Each of the 40 lessons becomes the stated reason behind a rule or a part's `why`, or is recorded as retired; a reason that belongs on a hashed page is queued for slice Z. A reason for a B7 page goes to a decision record instead, so nothing edits a B7 page after its recording; a later edit waits past M3 or comes with a budgeted re-record. The format defines how an accepted record changes: a dated "Amended" paragraph, the form 0004 already uses. A `decisions` check holds every record to the format and accepts that form. | worker; driver for the disagreements record and the check | Criteria 23–24 |
| B6 | Wrappers | Each core procedure that can run now gets a thin wrapper in the Agent Skills format: a description that says when to fire and when not, naming the wrappers next to it, and a body that points at the canonical page. Declared for Claude Code as a local plugin; Codex CLI's reading of skills is checked at the slice and stated. A check fails a wrapper whose page is missing, a core procedure with no wrapper that is not on the check's list of pages waiting for M4 or M8, and a lane draft or waiting page with one. Selection cases on `claude plugin eval`: at least one should-fire and one should-not-fire prompt per wrapper, results recorded. Their format is the one M4's eval system adopts, and `evals/README.md` says so. | driver | Criteria 21–22 |
| B7 | Proof attempts | For adversarial document review and for debugging: two cases each, drawn from M1 and M2's real work. Each playbook's two-line text is written and committed before its baseline runs. First a bare-only baseline, 10 runs per case. If the bare model passes 8 or more of 10 in both cases, the page is cut and the baseline kept as the reason. If it passes 5 or fewer in either case, record three arms at 22 runs per case, 132 runs per playbook, 44 per arm. In between, the page stays unproven, and the baseline is written as a failed-attempt record. If the stop rule's retry-path projection defers the adversarial-review recording, that page stays unproven with its baseline run and its recording deferred to M9. The baseline and the recording run in separate sessions, and adversarial review records one case per session, about 140 runs each with its dispatched sessions and probes, so no session passes the 150-run cap. B7 starts on a page only after that page's instruction review and the maintainer's read of its batch, which is recorded as a dated line under "Results" and which B7 checks for. Adversarial review is graded on its protocol, and its page records that it stays thin by design. Each B7 case names its own arm files: the playbook under test, its two-line text and a fixed copy of whatever contract text the arm needs. So these cases depend on no shared arm file, and on no file slice Z edits. They do depend on the harness and the graders, which no slice after A changes, and on the two playbook pages, which slice Z does not edit. A pass flips only the proof keys, which the hash ignores, so the record stays current. Each adversarial-review run dispatches a second session, which counts toward the 1,000. | driver | Criteria 25, 61 |

Each B2 page's `prove-by`. The rule: the first milestone whose program section runs the procedure on real work, never before M4, when the eval system lands. Development procedures default to M6, where real fixes go upstream and each procedure used gets its eval case. Templates, tutorials and reference pages carry no proof field. The fix path's review steps and provider steps are not written in M3: they cannot run before M4 and M8, which build them, and the private ledger maps them there.

### Sub-batch C: the machinery and the kit

Every slice here is machinery: its own branch, the failing case first, a pull request, CI on three systems.

| Order | Slice | Work | Tier | Done when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| C1 | Launcher and baselines | `wslib/launch.sh` grows from starting `ws.py` to starting every hook, git hook, check and CI step, in UTF-8 mode, with one interpreter start per call; the Windows store stub is skipped, and Windows CI steps call it through `bash`. Today the launcher starts Python twice, because its minimum-version probe is a separate start. The probe moves into the one start, and still exits with install advice when the version is too low or there is no Python. A guard's wiring refuses when the launcher fails for any reason; a reminder's passes, and so does the stop gate's, so a broken Python can never keep a session from finishing. One baseline file of tolerated findings per check, recorded as finding fingerprints, all empty here, read the same way at the commit and in CI, so an adopting team can start with known findings and fail only on new ones. The baseline files are machinery paths. | driver | Criteria 26–27, 30, 57 |
| C2 | The case runner | Cases build scratch repositories and seed leases with live and dead process ids. The runner times each hook's real call through the launcher and prints the median. A resolved `git push` and a `gh` write are timed as rows of their own. On Linux and macOS it fails a before-tool or after-edit hook above 200 milliseconds, a session-start hook above 2 seconds against a local remote (its fast-forward fetches origin, and the fetch gets a timeout), and a stop hook above the stop gate's 20 seconds; on Windows it reports. `test_cases_bite` iterates the manifest instead of a fixed list, and runs every part's cases against a stub that allows everything and finds nothing, and against one that refuses everything. | driver | Criteria 28, 42 |
| C3 | Settings, guards and the commit | It opens with an amendment to decision record 0004: the before-shell event gains, as runner-supplied needs, the kind of working copy (main or linked worktree) of the folder each git call acts on, as the parser resolves it, the session, the lease holder, and the target repository. For `gh api` it is the literal owner/repo in the path, with placeholders filled from `GH_REPO`, then gh's set-default value, then the only remote. For other gh calls it is `-R` or `--repo`, then `GH_REPO`, then set-default, then the only remote. With two or more remotes and no default set, the target is unresolved and a gh write is refused, since gh then picks a remote by its own rules. gh's folder follows the parser's folder rules, and list entries name host, owner and repository. For `git push` it is the destination URL git would really use, worked out from the resolved folder's config plus the command's `-c` and `GIT_CONFIG_*` values, `pushurl`, `insteadOf` and `pushInsteadOf` included. The guard asks git wherever git can answer (`git remote get-url --push --all`, `git config`, with the command's `-c` and environment values), reimplements longest-prefix `insteadOf` and `pushInsteadOf` only for URL arguments, judges every destination a push reaches, and models `push.default` and the upstream branch when no refspec is given. Anything the resolver does not model is unresolved. A local-path or `file://` remote, by git's rules (`host:path` is ssh), counts as inside the instance, and a gh write with no repository target counts as unlisted. Allow and deny lists for both agent tools: the deny list covers the secrets files, and the allow list names the documentation domains web fetch may read. A linked worktree gets a copy of the ignored local settings file from a `post-checkout` git hook that runs on `git worktree add`, so its hooks and permissions match the main copy's; the 0004 amendment names the hook. The repository's and the kit's `.gitignore` gain `.claude/settings.local.json`, which today only the maintainer's global ignore covers. A discard guard, as a new part, active only while another live session is on record, so a person with one session sees no change, as decision record 0005 promises. "Another live session is on record" means the lease is held by a live session other than this one; sessions in worktrees take no lease and so do not count. While that holds: hard reset, `reset --keep`, whole-tree checkout or restore, `checkout -f`, `switch -f`, `read-tree -u --reset`, forced clean and stash writes are refused in the main working copy; in the main working copy, rebase, cherry-pick, a rebasing pull and any of them with autostash are refused unless the session holds the lease; other commands in linked worktrees pass. From any working copy, `stash drop`, `pop` and `clear`, which reach the stash every worktree shares, and `worktree remove --force` or `prune` of a worktree the session is not in, are refused. An unresolved command is refused. If the guard finds a parser gap, it handles the gap in its own part file and queues the parser fix for slice Z. The 0005 amendment states one limit: worktree sessions are recorded nowhere, so nothing stops the lease holder from dropping a worktree session's stash or removing its worktree; M5's worktree work may record them. A secret scan as a part, on the lines a commit adds and in CI over the whole history. The commit-subject check, with the accepted formats read from the manifest, conventional commits by default. Every check's finding names the file, the rule's page and the fix or re-run command. | driver | Criteria 29, 31–34, 58, 64 |
| C4 | Push and merge | A push from the main working copy by a session that does not hold the lease is refused. A merge helper: `ws.py merge` refuses unless every check the branch rule requires passed on exactly the pull request's head commit, then merges with a merge commit, never a squash, so pinned anchors stay reachable, deletes the branch and fast-forwards main under the conditions M2's session-start fast-forward uses. Its `gh` calls are a build function and a parse function, tested on recorded output; no environment variable or argument can redirect them. `no_admin_merge` also refuses `gh pr merge --squash` or `--rebase`, and a `gh api` merge whose `merge_method` is not `merge`. The README's setup and the kit tell the person to allow merge commits only in the repository settings, which also covers merges from the web page. | driver | Criteria 35–36 |
| C5 | Reminders and guards | Codex CLI 0.169 appears to offer both new events: its binary names them (inferred from strings in the binary, not from a run). Before building, one recorded run of each new event is made in each agent tool, which confirms it or not. If Codex lacks one, the slice stops and proposes the revision, as template rule 7 asks. An `after-edit` event in both adapters, and the chain reminder on it. A force-push guard for the default branch, covering `+` and `:` refspecs, `--mirror`, `-f` while on it, and `--force` in every form; `--force-with-lease` to another branch is allowed. The outward-write guard described in the key points, with its list of repositories in a file of its own, `outward-repos.json`, which the manifest points to, owned by the instance and never synced. On an unlisted repository it allows a named list of `gh` read subcommands only. A `gh api` call is a read only when its method is GET, or when it has no method, no field flags and no `--input`; its target is the path's owner/repo, as C3 resolves it, and a write whose path names no repository counts as unlisted. On every repository, this one included, every `gh api graphql` call is refused, since node ids name no repository and nothing here needs GraphQL; and on a reviews path, `--input` and `@file` fields are refused and `event` must be a literal other than APPROVE. `git push` to a remote or URL of an unlisted repository is refused; the edit guard refuses an agent's edit to that list, so only a person, from a terminal, can add a repository. The force-push and outward-write guards refuse a command the parser reports as unresolved, as the discard guard does, each with a case; the heredoc commit and pull-request idioms and `cd "$D" && make` stay allowed, with a case each. A push of a substituted branch name is refused with a message that points to `git push -u origin HEAD`. The force-push and outward-write guards work out each push's real destination and refspecs as C3 says, so `--all`, `--branches`, `--delete`, `--prune` and glob refspecs are judged by what they would push. They and the discard guard refuse `git -c`, `git config` and environment writes to every key A0 lists; refuse `git remote add` and `set-url` to an unlisted URL; and count `gh repo set-default`, `git remote`, `git branch -u` or `--set-upstream-to`, `switch` or `checkout` with tracking, and `worktree add` from a remote branch earlier in the same command line as unresolved. Arguments that `xargs` or `find -exec {}` supply count as unresolved for the guard that decides on the command. The edit guard and the shell guards refuse an agent's write under any `.git` path segment, a linked worktree's `.git` file and `src/*/.git/` included, and to the repository list, whether by an edit tool or by a shell write the parser reports; `.github/`, `.githooks/` and `.gitignore` stay editable. The guards expand a git alias from config at hook time, only for a name that is not a built-in command, as git does; an alias starting with `!` is unresolved, and so is a subcommand that is neither built in nor an alias at hook time. A reported write to a git config file is refused like a `.git` write: `~/.gitconfig`, `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/git/config`, `~/.config/git/config`, and any file an `include.path` or `includeIf` names. `HOME`, `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`, `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL`, `GIT_CONFIG_SYSTEM` and `GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM` set on a guarded git command count as config writes. A stale `index.lock` is refused like any `.git` write, with a message telling a person to remove it. The README says an interpreter can still write. Each has a case. `no_admin_merge` adopts the same rule. The README states what stays out of reach: scripts, interpreters, aliases in shell configuration, gh aliases, and PowerShell, which Codex CLI may use on Windows. A parser gap found here is handled as in C3. Secrets kept out of the agent's shell: environment dumps and reads of the secrets files are refused, and `ws.py with-secrets` passes named values to one child process only, refusing a shell, `env` or `printenv` as that child; the README states what remains possible. A stop gate on a new `stop` event: when a session with its own entries in the ledger tries to finish, the runner runs `check` and a named fast subset of the tests within 20 seconds, refuses once while either fails, then reports. The ledger records the session of each entry, and decision record 0005 is amended for it; the gate does not see edits made through the shell, and says so. The handoff note's loader, following the catalog's marker rule: writing a handoff note leaves a marker keyed by branch name, in the working copy's git folder, written by `ws.py handoff`, which the handoff page tells the agent to run, since agents may not write in the git folder themselves; writing again updates that marker in place and never touches another. A marker is pending until its branch merges or `ws.py handoff --done` clears it. At session start, the loader shows the note that one pending marker names, and with two or more pending markers it shows none and lists them. Decision record 0004 is amended for the two new events and the loader. | driver | Criteria 37–41, 59, 65 |
| C6 | Not inherited | No guard turns off when any single environment variable is unset. One opt-out is intended and stated: the `deny_list` check warns and checks nothing when its variable is unset and the default file is missing, because CI and an outside clone have no private list. A killed commit check leaves no temporary file, worktree or lock behind. | driver | Criterion 46 |
| C7 | Clones and checkouts | A nightly cold-clone job: clone fresh, follow the README's marked setup steps and nothing else, run `selftest`, `check` and the tests. It also runs on a pull request, and on a push to main, that changes the README or a setup page, since doc changes go straight to main. A pinned-repository initializer that probes every remote in parallel with prompts off and a timeout, initializes those that answer, and never touches an existing checkout. The link check gains exemptions as source-and-target pairs with a reason, and opt-in backticked paths. | driver | Criteria 43–45, 66 |
| C8 | The kit | Before building, read AI-DLC's packager, which projects one core of hooks and agent tiers into several agent tools. Take its projection where it fits the tier binding and the hook wiring, and credit what was taken in the decision record. A kit manifest lists each kit file with its class (synced, seeded once, never synced) and its hash, and names every seeded item. It also carries each synced page's proof state as of the kit version, which the `prove-by` and `eval_record` checks read in an instance, since milestone and eval records are never synced. `ws.py adopt PATH` creates an instance, with an empty list of repositories for the outward-write guard; `ws.py drift` fails on a hand edit to a synced file; an upgrade replaces synced files whose hash matches the instance's lock, and refuses on drift. A decision record holds the classes and the kill rules: three divergent copies of a synced procedure in a quarter demote it to seeded; no outside adopter in two quarters demotes the kit to a plain template. | driver | Criterion 47 |
| C9 | The two-session demonstration | It runs in a scratch clone of this repository whose remote is a local bare repository, so "main" below is that scratch repository's main, never this one's. Two headless agent sessions start at once on the same main working copy. The second is refused there and works in a worktree. Both commits reach main, and neither holds the other's files. The transcripts are kept as records, with a replay test. | driver | Criterion 48 |

C1, C2 and C7 branch from main and start at once, as sub-batch A's note says. C7 goes right after C1, so its nights accumulate while the rest is built. C3 to C6, C8 and C9 start after sub-batch A merges. Sub-batches B and C run in parallel sessions, each in its own worktree. Worktree sessions take no lease; they stay apart because each slice owns its branch and its files.

### Closing

| Order | Slice | Work | Tier | Done when |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Z | Contract, index and recordings | The queued edits, from the list under "Results": index rows for every new page, chain and part; the request map and the guide linked from the contract; the definition of done brought in line with the new record types; the fix path's gates merged into their own section of `fix-a-bug.md`, with its proof line; lesson reasons for hashed pages. The instruction-file review runs on each. The manifest's version goes to 0.4.0. Then R2, 135 runs, as R1, in one session. Then, in a second session, the routed arm: the guided arm's files, every page under `docs/` that the index links to, unproven pages included, and the wrappers enabled, 15 runs in each case, 45 in all, written to a one-time record. An arm that misses its margin in R2 or the routed run is recorded once more, 45 runs, in its own session, and judged pooled. None of these edits touches a file B7's cases hash. Each of the three fix-a-bug cases' requests grants any approval a merged gate asks for, so an unattended run does not halt on it, and the case files say so. R2 therefore runs a different request from R1, and its report says the comparison is confounded by that change. On R1's retry path, no margin re-record is left: a margin miss here is a stop. | driver | Criteria 53–54 |

After slice Z, the program's outside checks run before sign-off: CI, a fresh-session red team, and private attack scripts kept outside this repository, written by a session that built nothing here, against each new guard. The tag `v0.4.0` follows sign-off.

## Success criteria

Sub-batch A:

1. A case can name its own arm files, and the three fix-a-bug cases' guided arm holds the rule pages matching the paths their acceptance tests and graders name. (V1)
2. `run.py --baseline` writes a record that `eval_record` never counts as passing. (V1)
3. `eval_record` fails a proven playbook whose cases, pooled, have fewer than 40 runs in an arm, or whose guided arm does not beat its two-line arm at p < 0.05. (V1)
4. `eval_record` fails a new record whose guided arm passes more than 3 runs fewer, pooled across the playbook's cases, than the previous record's guided arm, or than the guided arm of the record the playbook's `proven-by` names. The engine supplies both records, reading the anchor from its commit-pinned path. When arm sizes differ, pass rates are compared, with a case where a pooled arm passes on count and fails on rate. Each side is scaled by 2 if it has 45 runs and by 1 if pooled, and the new record fails when its scaled count is below the old one minus the margin: 6 if either side is pooled, 3 otherwise. A margin runs over the cases present in both records. A case covers both sides pooled. (V1)
5. The review-protocol and diagnosis graders each have a test with a passing and a failing run. (V1)
6. `python3 evals/grade.py --recorded` passes after the harness changes. (V3)
7. `page_kinds` fails a page missing a front-matter key its kind requires, a value outside its vocabulary, or an opening out of order. It fails when it checks no pages. It fails a page past its review date, at the commit and in CI, with the cases `freshness` had. It fails a purpose sentence over 25 words, except on an approved plan, an accepted decision record or a signed-off milestone record, with a case for each exemption. `freshness` is in `retired`, and no manifest entry names it. (V2)
8. A page under `archive/` is not found by `rg` run with its default settings from the repository root, and the link and page checks skip it. (V1)
9. Every path listed in the index's cross-cutting chains exists; the check fails on one that does not. (V2)
10. The Copilot and opencode pointer files each name `AGENTS.md` and the rule pages; a check fails when one stops doing so. (V2)
11. `AGENTS.md` no longer says `src/` holds one repository per folder, and the index has no "plan being built now" row pointing at a finished plan. (V2)
12. `rules` fails a rule tagged with a part that is not in the manifest, or with a case that does not exist. `python3 ws.py rules` prints every rule with its tag and a count per tag. (V2)
13. `loaded_size` fails when `AGENTS.md`, `CLAUDE.md` and the pointer files exceed the manifest's ceiling for them (6,000 bytes), or the wrappers' descriptions exceed theirs (9,000 bytes). (V2)
14. Every playbook and how-to has a `proof` field, and every unproven one a `prove-by` milestone. A check fails an unproven page or section whose `prove-by` milestone record is signed off, a `prove-by` newly set, against the merge base, to any milestone in `building:`, and a `prove-by` later than at the merge base with no failed-attempt record under `evals/runs/` naming the page, with a case for each. A page marked proven without a `proven-by` link, or without a passing per-playbook record, fails. A proven section needs the same evidence as a proven page. A change to an existing `proven-by` anchor without an approved change entry fails, and so does a renamed page whose proof keys change. A move later needs a failed-attempt record for the milestone it moves away from, and an old failed attempt does not license a second move, with a case. A record added while its milestone was not in `building:` licenses no move, with a case. `building:` is a list, and a `prove-by` newly set to any listed milestone fails. Every anchor sha is a full sha and an ancestor of the commit being checked: a branch-only anchor passes on its pull request, and a sha missing from history fails. A new anchor names the record that passed, and an anchor change needs the entry A3 defines. A case may name `page#section`, and a section's test pools only its cases. The README's proven and unproven table matches the pages. Decision record 0006 exists. (V2)
15. R1 passes the gate, and its guided arm passes no more than 3 runs fewer, pooled, than M2's closing recording (40 of 45), which is both the previous record and the one `fix-a-bug.md`'s `proven-by` names. After one re-record of the guided arm, the pooled 90 runs pass no fewer than 74. (V3)

Sub-batch B:

16. The private port ledger maps every item the batch table gives M3 to a public page, a later milestone or a recorded reason. Its check prints 0 unmapped items. (V5)
17. Every commit after B1 merges that adds or changes a guidance page carries an `Instructions-review:` trailer naming a review record that exists, or a skip reason from the listed set. The commit check refuses a commit without one, and the local pre-push check refuses a push that holds one. ## Verification

- **V1:** `python3 -m unittest discover -b tests` passes, including the test each criterion names.
- **V2:** `python3 ws.py selftest`, `python3 ws.py check` and `python3 -m unittest discover -b tests` all exit 0.
- **V3:** `evals/run.py` writes the records under `evals/runs/`; `python3 evals/pool.py` prints each comparison; `python3 evals/grade.py --recorded` and `python3 ws.py check --phase ci` pass.
- **V4:** `gh pr checks <number>` shows every required check passing.
- **V5:** the private port ledger's own check, run on the maintainer's machine, prints 0 unmapped items. The ledger and its check live beside the private catalog, outside every repository.
- **V6:** `claude plugin eval` runs each wrapper's selection cases. The exact command is confirmed at slice B6 and written into `evals/README.md`.
- **V7:** `python3 -m unittest tests.test_cases_bite -b` passes.
- **V8:** `gh run list --workflow cold-clone --event schedule --limit 3` shows three runs on consecutive days, all successful, the last after slice Z's merge.
- **V9:** the private attack scripts, run on the maintainer's machine from outside the repository, against a fresh clone of main.

## Recovery

Halt and report. Do not work around a failing case or loosen a check to pass. If a hook locks the session out, stop; the maintainer repairs it from a terminal, per [changing the machinery](../changing-the-machinery.md#traps). If a slice finds that a later slice's criterion is wrong, it proposes the revision in its report, as the template's rule 7 asks.

## Stop rule

Stop the plan and report when any of these happens:

- R1 fails criterion 15, after the one re-record a margin miss allows. One recovery is allowed: revert the guidance only, and record again, with no re-record of its own. After it, a margin miss in R2 or the routed arm is a stop, not a re-record, since that path has planned 799 runs before probes and dispatched sessions. The guidance is the arm files' text, the contract, the index, the rule pages added to the guided arm, and `fix-a-bug.md`'s text and its front matter other than `proof`, `proven-by` and the anchor, which stay. The harness, the graders and the shell parser stay. If that recording passes, these criteria move to slice Z: 1 (rule pages in the guided arm), 7 for the reverted pages, 9 and 11 (the index and contract edits), and 12 for the register's rows on reverted pages; criterion 14 stays in sub-batch A. The checks behind them ship with a named exemption for each reverted file, which slice Z removes. The report says M2's open question stays confounded. If the second recording also fails, publish both results and stop.
- On the retry path, the projected total of eval runs passes 1,000. The session projects once when the retry is chosen, before B7's recordings, and may then defer B7's adversarial-review recording to M9, its `prove-by` milestone. It projects again before R2; if the total still passes 1,000 there, stop for the maintainer's decision.
- R2 fails criterion 53, after the one re-record a margin miss allows. Publish the result and stop.
- The routed arm fails criterion 54, after the one re-record a margin miss allows. Publish the result and stop; the unproven pages and the wrappers stay out of the index and the plugin until a person decides.
- The instruction-file review records the same class of defect in more than a third of the pages ported so far, counted after each batch. Stop and fix the rewrite brief before the next batch.
- The engine, a part or a CI test needs a third-party package.
- More than five catalog items cannot be made generic without a private fact. Fewer are left out, each with its reason in the private ledger.
- A slice goes past two fix rounds on the same failure.
- The eval runs reach 1,000 agent runs, or the selection runs 300, before the plan ends. Margin re-records, probes, failed runs and the sessions an adversarial-review run dispatches count toward the 1,000.

A part that cannot be made safe on Windows within its slice ships for macOS and Linux, says so in the README, and does not weaken the guard. That is not a stop.

## Limits

- Each slice is built in its own worktree under `.claude/worktrees/`, on its own branch; sub-batch A shares one. No builder checks out a branch in the main working copy.
- Eval-harness work is committed with `--no-verify` before the paid runs, as the [machinery page's trap](../changing-the-machinery.md#traps) describes.
- Each recording or proof session is capped at 150 agent runs, probes, retries and dispatched sessions included. R2 and the routed arm run in separate sessions, as do each B7 baseline and its recording, and each margin re-record. After a usage limit, a person or session reruns with `run.py --resume`; nothing restarts on its own.
- Agents push the branch, open a draft pull request, and publish and merge it once every required check passes.
- Unattended runs are limited to the eval and selection recordings, the recorded tool runs in C5, and C9's two headless sessions. None of them may push to this repository's main, publish or merge. C9's sessions push only to the main of their scratch repository's local bare remote.
- Builders read the private port catalog and ledger, and nothing of the work workspace. A builder that needs more asks for a walled reader, which writes to the private catalog.
- Maintainer setup before A4: `gh auth status` passes, the Claude Code command line tool is signed in, and the repository settings allow merge commits only. Before C5: Codex CLI signed in, for its recorded runs.

## Records this plan must leave

- Decision records 0006 (what proven means), the kit's, the disagreements', each ported workspace decision, and the amendments to 0004 and 0005.
- The proven and unproven table in the README.
- The R1 and R2 recordings, the routed arm's one-time record, the baselines, any proof recordings, and the selection results.
- The instruction-review records, and the trailers that name them.
- The two-session demonstration's records.
- A report per slice under "Results", with each claim marked verified or inferred, and the list of hashed-file edits queued for slice Z.
- `docs/milestones/m3.md`, with the always-loaded size before and after, what was deleted from it, the final budget, and the outside checks' outcomes, plus entries in `CHANGELOG.md` and `EVIDENCE.md`.
- Question-log rows for anything a builder had to ask.

## Later

- **The review orchestrator's machinery, the review mark and draft-only pull requests.** M4. The pages that wait for it get wrappers then.
- **Eval marks at push and the eval stage in CI.** M4, adopting B6's selection-case format.
- **Machine-state records.** M4 or M5, named in the ledger. Deferred with the maintainer's yes of 2026-11-17.
- **Environment slots, code maps, verifying a change in a local environment, and the doc-coverage and doc-symbol checks.** M5, against real code. The local-environment page was deferred with the maintainer's yes of 2026-11-17.
- **The CI job that detects what changed, to skip machines a change does not need.** M5. Here every pull request runs every job in minutes; the saving appears once pinned code makes CI heavy.
- **Deny entries for destructive tracker and wiki operations.** M8, when the first tracker and wiki tools arrive. Nothing here can call one yet.
- **Removing a session's worktree after its work lands.** A `session-end` reminder; it waits for real worktree use in M5.
- **Worktrees reading pinned repositories from the main copy.** Matters once pinned repositories exist, in M5.
- **Trap tables for a tracker and a git host, and the schema-drift check.** M8. The tracker and git-host spines get wrappers then.
- **The request door.** M9. **Release and rollback.** M11.

## Red team

Round 1, 2026-11-17: a fresh session given the draft at `f85fa9a`, the program plan, the private port catalog and this repository. Two helpers at the driver tier read the catalog and the code separately, and a fresh scratch clone passed `selftest`, `check`, `check --phase ci`, `grade.py --recorded` and the tests. The verdicts are decided. The second draft applied each action to the body; criteria 1 to 54 keep their numbers, and 55 to 60 are new. Findings 18 and 37 change the program's M3 list or a decision record; the maintainer said yes to both on 2026-11-17. Round 2 found actions 1, 4, 5, 8, 27, 28 and 38 only partly applied; the third draft completed them, as noted in those rows.

| # | Finding | Verdict | Action |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The routed arm cannot be added in slice Z without staling every record. `run.py` accepts exactly three arms, the harness is in every case's hash, and `arms.json` is shared, so its files enter every case's hash. B7's proof records, about 240 runs, would go stale at close, unbudgeted. | Accepted | A0 builds support for a fourth arm and every grader the plan needs, and hashes per-case arm files into their own case only. Slice Z changes no harness file. Completed in the third draft (R2-1, R2-4). |
| 2 | The shared shell parser lives in `no_stage_all.py`, which the eval hashes, yet C3 and C5 must change it. Decision 0004 already says moving it waits for a recording. | Accepted | A0 moves the parser into `wslib/`, before R1, with a 0004 row for each part that loads it. |
| 3 | The `before-shell` event carries only the command text, so the discard guard, the push-lease check and the publish guard cannot tell the main copy from a worktree, who holds the lease, or which repository. Decision 0002 rejected teaching the parser where a command runs. | Accepted | A 0004 amendment, before C3, adds those facts to `before-shell` as runner-supplied needs. Each guard's criteria test the fact it uses. |
| 4 | The guard criteria miss forms the current parser lets through: `env` and `env \| grep`, `GH_REPO=… gh pr merge`, `git -c alias.x=… x`, `git $x`, piping into `sh`, `+main` and `:main` refspecs, `--mirror`, a bare `-f` while on main, `gh api` merge and review calls, `switch -f`, `checkout -f`, `read-tree -u --reset`, `reset --keep`. | Accepted | Each form becomes a case, either refused or listed as a stated limit in the README. Completed in the third draft (R2-16). |
| 5 | The publish-and-approve guard is narrower than the program's rule that outward writes are the person's. It leaves `gh pr create`, `gh pr comment`, `gh pr review --comment` and `gh issue create` or `comment` open on any repository. The list of allowed repositories also sits in a file an agent can edit. | Accepted | C5 refuses every write subcommand, and `gh api` calls that write, on a repository not listed. The list is instance-owned and never synced, and `ws.py adopt` creates it empty. Criterion 39 names each command. Completed in the third draft (R2-17). |
| 6 | `ws.py with-secrets` hands the values to any child process, so a child of `env`, `printenv` or `sh -c 'echo …'` prints them into the transcript. Criterion 40 tests only the calling shell. | Accepted | Refuse known printers and shells as the child, test it, and state the remaining limit in the README. |
| 7 | The stop gate cannot finish in time. The tests take about 35 seconds on macOS, and the Codex wiring sets a 30-second timeout. The ledger is kept per working copy, not per session, so the gate fires for sessions that edited nothing and misses edits made through the shell. | Accepted | The gate runs `check` and a named fast subset of the tests within a stated time. It keys on the session's own ledger entries and says it does not see shell edits. Criterion 28 times real calls. |
| 8 | Criterion 41's "Codex is covered if it offers the event" is the escape clause M2's red team removed. The recorded Codex runs show only the before-tool, session-start and session-end events, while criterion 37 requires `after-edit` from Codex with no hedge. | Accepted | Before C5, record one Codex run per new event. The plan then names, now, each event Codex lacks, and criteria 37 and 41 agree. Completed in the third draft (R2-18). |
| 9 | "Loses no comparison it won" adds nothing to today's gate. The gate already requires guided above bare in each case and the pooled test above two-line, so it cannot catch a slide like M2's 45 to 40. It also needs the previous record, which a part's snapshot does not provide. | Accepted | Compare the guided arm with its own previous count, within a stated margin. The engine supplies the previous record. Applies to criteria 4, 15, 53 and the stop rule. |
| 10 | The no-harm test is weak. Routed 33 against guided 40 of 45 gives p = 0.052 and passes, and a real drop to 75% is caught 43% of the time. The key points say "does worse", which is a different rule. The routed arm also leaves out the wrapper descriptions every session loads, the likeliest harm after M2. R2 plus the routed arm is 180 runs, over the 150-run session cap. | Accepted | Use a fixed margin (routed at least guided minus 3, pooled) in both places. Run the routed arm with the wrappers enabled. Split R2 and the routed arm across two sessions. |
| 11 | B7's proofs will likely end unproven, with no slack. Guided at 36 of 40 needs two-line at 29 or fewer, which happens about 45% of the time if two-line passes 75%. Forty is exactly the minimum, so one lost run fails the gate. | Accepted | 22 runs per case (44 per arm). Each two-line text is fixed and committed before its baseline runs. |
| 12 | The adversarial-review proof grades the critic's quality ("names the flaw, gives a verdict"). The page deliberately gives no method guidance, because guidance anchors the critic. Its value is the protocol, so the test pushes toward cutting the page or padding it. | Accepted | Grade the protocol: a fresh session was dispatched, the findings file exists, the reply gives only its path and verdict, and a triage table exists. The page records that it stays thin by design. |
| 13 | Several B2 and B3 pages would duplicate pages already here: the fix path against `fix-a-bug.md` (proven and hashed), the ladder against `size-the-work.md`, delegation against `orchestration.md`, the plan template against `hand-off-plan.md`, and the question log. | Accepted | B2 and B3 gain a column: "merges into X" or "new page". A merge into a hashed page is queued for slice Z. Applied in prose, not as a column (R2-19). |
| 14 | Every core procedure gets a wrapper, but several cannot run until M4 or M8 (the review orchestrator and the tracker and git-host spines). The draft's own reason for denying lane drafts a wrapper applies to them too. None has a bare-model baseline, which the pattern asks for before a skill ships. | Accepted | Wrappers only for core pages that can run now. The others wait, as lane drafts do, and the wrapper check lists them by name. |

Round 3, 2026-11-17: a fresh session given the third draft at `6bfba59`, the program plan, the private port catalog and this repository. It built nothing. It read each accepted action of rounds 1 and 2 against the body, read the code each claim rests on, computed the margin rule's false-stop rate and power exactly, and tried two guard bypasses in a throwaway repository. In a scratch clone, `selftest`, `check` with the private deny list, `check --phase ci` and `grade.py --recorded` passed, and 214 unit tests passed with 2 skipped. Marks: **V** means a file, a command or a computation was checked; **I** means inferred. The verdicts are decided. The fourth draft applied each action to the body, as noted in each row; the maintainer said yes to rows R3-1 and R3-13 on 2026-11-18.

Of the 77 accepted rows in rounds 1 and 2, 73 are fully applied. Four are partly applied: round-1 rows 5 and 15 and round-2 rows 8 and 25. Their remaining work is in R3-7, R3-14, R3-2 and R3-17, and those two tables stay as written. This round does more than finish earlier work: R3-1, R3-3 to R3-9 and R3-11 are defects of a kind no earlier row covers. Under the program's stop rule the review loop continues, so a fourth draft and a round-4 check come before approval.

| # | Finding | Verdict | Action |
|---|---|---|---|
| R3-1 | B7 cannot succeed without staling its own record. `covered_files` always hashes the case's playbook, and the hash ignores only the `last-reviewed:` line (V, `parts/eval_record.py`). A page B7 proves must change `proof`, add `proven-by` and drop "unproven" from its purpose line. Each edit changes the hash, so criterion 61 fails in exactly the branch where B7 passes. A re-record costs about 132 runs per page, unbudgeted. Program M4 slice 8 has the same trap (I). | Accepted; needs the maintainer's yes, because it narrows what counts as a change to a playbook | A0: the runner strips the `proof`, `proven-by` and `prove-by` keys from a page before an arm reads it, and the hash ignores the same keys, so the hashed text is exactly the text tested. A3: the unproven label lives in those keys only, not in the purpose line; that amends A3's page form. A case shows a proven/unproven flip leaves a record current. B7 and criterion 61 say so. Applied: key points, A0, A3, B7, criteria 61 and 67. |
| R3-2 | The run budget does not fit the retry path. The base 619 is right (V). R1's retry follows R1's one margin re-record, so that path costs 619 + 45 + 135 = 799 (V). Any margin re-record of R2 or the routed arm then passes 800. A margin miss, not the Fisher gate, is the likely R1 failure: the gate fails on noise 0.08% of the time (V). The plan does not say whether probes and failed runs count; past recordings used 4 to 9 extra runs each (V, milestone records). Each B7 adversarial-review run also starts a second agent (I). | Accepted | Keep the 800 stop. State the retry path's cost, 799, and that in that path a margin miss in R2 or the routed arm is a stop, not a re-record, and the retry gets no re-record of its own. Probes, failed runs and dispatched sub-sessions count toward the 800. Key points, Stop rule and the budget sentence change to match. Applied: key points, Z, Stop rule. |
| R3-3 | Criteria 31 and 64 contradict C3. C3 lets linked worktrees run the rebase family. Criteria 31 and 64 refuse it to any session that does not hold the lease, and worktree sessions never hold it. Read literally, every worktree session's `git pull --rebase` is refused while someone holds the main copy (V, text). | Accepted | Scope the rebase-family rule in criteria 31 and 64 to the main working copy. Add a case that allows it in a linked worktree while another live session holds the lease. Applied: C3, criteria 31 and 64. |
| R3-4 | Linked worktrees share `refs/stash`. A stash made in the main copy was listed and dropped from a linked worktree (V, throwaway repository). A worktree session can also `git worktree remove --force` another session's worktree (I). C3 lists neither. | Accepted | C3 refuses stash `drop`, `pop` and `clear`, and `worktree remove --force` or `prune` of another worktree, from any working copy, while another live session is on record. Criterion 31 gets a case for each. Applied: A0, C3, criterion 31. |
| R3-5 | The guard judges the session's folder, not the folder a command acts on: the runner reads the event's `cwd` (V, `wslib/events.py`; decision 0002). From a worktree, `git -C <main> reset --hard`, `cd <main> && git checkout -- .` or `GIT_DIR=… git clean -fdx` would pass (I). The force-push rule "`-f` while on it" rests on the same fact. | Accepted | The 0004 amendment defines the working copy as the folder the git call acts on. The parser resolves `-C`, `--git-dir`, `--work-tree`, `GIT_DIR`, `GIT_WORK_TREE` and a leading `cd` or `pushd`; anything else that moves the target counts as unresolved. Criterion 58 gets a case for each. Applied: key points, A0, C3, criterion 58. |
| R3-6 | "Unresolved" is undefined, and either reading breaks something. The parser keeps `$(…)` and variables as plain words (V, parser run on common commands). If any such word counts, the always-on guards refuse the usual heredoc commit and pull-request commands and `git push origin "$(git branch --show-current)"`. If none counts, `git push origin "$(echo +main)"` passes. The plan states no README limit for scripts or interpreters (V, grep), and `no_admin_merge` stays fail-open (V). | Accepted | A0 defines unresolved as: the program word, the git or gh subcommand, or a guarded argument (refspec, `--repo`, `GH_REPO`, `-C`) that is a variable or a substitution. Message bodies (`-m`, `--body`, `--title`, `-F`) never count. The parser reads inside `$(…)` and backticks. Criterion 55 gets cases for the heredoc idioms (allowed) and a substitution in a refspec (refused). C5 states the remaining limits in the README. `no_admin_merge` adopts the same rule. Applied: A0, C5, criterion 55. |
| R3-7 | The outward-write guard has gaps, finishing round-1 row 5. `gh api` sends POST once a field flag is given (V, `gh api --help`), and the guard looks only for an explicit method. `gh api graphql` names its target by node id (I). The `repos/OWNER/REPO` path is not one of C3's target sources (V). `git push` to an unlisted remote or URL is not covered (V, grep). Criterion 39 tests a subset of write commands (V). Round 4, 2026-11-18: a fresh session given the fourth draft at `bc1d016`, the program plan at its matching commit, the private port catalog and this repository. It built nothing. It checked each round-3 action against the body and the program plan, re-checked the round-1 and round-2 actions the fourth draft touched, ran parser probes in a scratch clone, and recomputed the statistics and budgets. In that clone, `selftest`, `check` with the private deny list, `check --phase ci` and `grade.py --recorded` passed, and 214 unit tests passed with 2 skipped. Marks: **V** means a file, a command or a computation was checked; **I** means inferred. The verdicts are decided. The fifth draft applied each action to the body, as noted in each row; the maintainer said yes to row R4-1 on 2026-11-18.

Sixteen of the 20 round-3 rows are fully applied, and the sampled round-1 and round-2 actions are intact. R3-5, R3-12, R3-13 and R3-19 are only partly applied; R4-10, R4-8 and R4-14 finish them. This round does more than finish earlier work: R4-2 to R4-5, R4-9, R4-11 to R4-13 and R4-15 are new defects, several of them brought in by the fourth draft's own changes. The review loop continues, so a fifth draft and a round-5 check come before approval.

The base path lands at about 735 to 834, and the retry path the stop rule allows at about 915 to 1,014, so the allowed recovery cannot finish under 800. | Accepted; needs the maintainer's yes, because it raises the run cap | Raise the eval stop from 800 to 1,000 runs and state the expected totals with probes and dispatched sessions included: about 780 base, about 1,000 on the retry path. The program's step list says each milestone sets `building:` when it starts and clears it at sign-off. Applied: A3, criterion 14; the program's step list. |

It strips only exact forms: `proof: proven` or `unproven`, `prove-by: M<n>`, one line of `proven-by` links, and the exact section line. `page_kinds` fails any variant.
